Results from pilot-scale CO2 capture testing using 30 wt% MEA at a
Waste-to-Energy facility: Optimisation through parametric analysis
Sai Hema Bhavya Vinjarapu a,∗, Randi Neerup a, Anders Hellerup Larsen a, Jens Kristian Jørsboe a,
Sebastian Nis Bay Villadsen a, Søren Jensen b, Jakob Lindkvist Karlsson b, Jannik Kappel c,
Henrik Lassen c, Peter Blinksbjerg c, Nicolas von Solms a, Philip Loldrup Fosbøl a

b Pentair Union Engineering A/S, Snaremosevej 27, 7000 Fredericia, Denmark

Keywords:
CO2 capture
Waste-to-Energy
A B S T R A C T
Post-combustion carbon capture is a well-established technology for removing CO2 from industrial emissions.
However, research is still underway to optimise the process and make it more energy efficient. The current
∗Corresponding author.
E-mail address: shbvin@kt.dtu.dk (S.H.B. Vinjarapu).

Specific reboiler duty (SRD)
Capture efficiency
Stripping intensity
work aims to present the results from pilot-scale (with a capacity of 1 tonne CO2/day) studies of CO2 capture
conducted at Amager Bakke, a Waste-to-Energy facility in Copenhagen, Denmark. The pilot operation is fully
automated, and the process control structure helps achieve multiple steady states at regular intervals. In
addition, the pilot is capable of several configurations which can assist in optimising the energy required for
solvent regeneration. The current work discusses the base case configuration of the pilot plant by employing
30 wt% MEA as the solvent. Experiments were conducted to analyse the influence of reboiler duty, solvent
flow rate, and gas flow rate on the pilot’s performance. The influence of these parameters on several aspects is
discussed in detail, and recommendations for the optimal operation of such plants are provided. An optimum
specific reboiler duty of 3.46 GJ/tonne CO2 is obtained at a solvent flow rate of 306 kg/h, flue gas flow rate
of 113 kg/h, and reboiler duty of 17.5 kW excluding heat loss.

1. Introduction
Carbon capture, utilisation, and storage (CCUS) is becoming in-
creasingly important in addressing climate change [1–3]. CCUS is a
crucial technology for reducing greenhouse gas emissions, especially
Subscripts

in industries that are difficult to decarbonise, such as cement and
steel production [4]. Post-combustion capture (PCC) is a promising
technology for reducing greenhouse gas emissions from large station-
ary industrial processes, such as heat and power generation [5]. PCC
involves the treatment of industrial gases consisting of low CO2 par-
tial pressures in large streams of non-condensable gases. The process
technology for acid gas removal by chemical absorption from natural
gas was developed in the 1920s [6]. Amine-based PCC offers various
advantages: it has high capture efficiency and selectivity, the tech-
nology is commercially available, and it can be retrofitted to existing
power plants [7–9]. Nevertheless, the technology has some limitations,
such as the high energy consumption required for the regeneration
process, the corrosive nature of the amine solvents, and the potential
for solvent degradation due to contaminants in the flue gas [8,10].
30 wt% Monoethanolamine (MEA), which is the most widely used
solvent, requires regeneration energy of 3.6–3.8 GJ/tonne CO2 [10,11]
which corresponds to an 11%–15% drop in the thermal efficiency of
the power plant including CO2 compression [12,13]. Regeneration of
MEA contributes to 70%–80% of the operating cost [14]. The energy
efficiency of the process could be improved by configuring the pilot for
optimisation [15], heat integration with the power plant, development
of new solvents [11,13,16–18], or a combination.
Pilot-scale testing of CO2 capture is essential for developing reliable
and cost-effective technologies. Pilot testing helps identify potential
problems and evaluate the performance of the capture process. Several
pilot-scale tests have been conducted at coal-fired power plants [10,19–
22] and natural gas-fired power plants [13,23–26] employing MEA as
the solvent. All of these studies were conducted without employing any
optimisation of a general absorption–desorption process for CO2 cap-
ture, which is essential for improving energy efficiency and reducing
the cost of the capture process. Literature on pilot-scale testing of CO2
capture employing various flue gas sources is presented in Table 1.

In addition to fossil fuel-based power plants, Waste-to-Energy (WtE)
facilities also produce CO2 emissions. These facilities use thermal treat-
ment of waste to produce energy. They are becoming increasingly
popular as an alternative to landfilling due to their environmental ben-
efits and use of renewable energy sources [41–44]. Shell’s CANSOLV
capture technology and solvent DC-103 pilot tests were conducted for
2000 operational hours at the Forum Oslo Varme WtE plant in Oslo,
Norway. However, limited information is available about the energy
consumed for solvent regeneration [45]. The Saga waste incineration
plant in Saga City, Japan has a CO2 capture plant that can capture
10 tonnes of CO2/day and use it for algae cultivation [46]. However,
information about the capture technology’s operation and performance
is scarce. The AVR Duiven WtE facility has implemented a commercial-
scale CO2 capture system with the ability to capture 100,000 tonnes
of CO2/yr [47]. A commercial-scale CO2 capture system is expected
to be operational at the Twence WtE facility in the Netherlands by
the end of 2023 [48]. These are the current full-scale and pilot-scale
activities related to CO2 capture in WtE facilities. WtE plants inte-
grated with CO2 capture are part of BECCS and contribute to negative
emissions [49]. Therefore, it is critical to conduct more pilot-scale
tests to develop reliable and cost-effective CO2 capture technologies for
full-scale implementation.
The current work aims to present baseline results from a pilot-scale
CO2 capture plant tested at Amager Bakke, Copenhagen, Denmark,
which is a WtE facility. The baseline experiments employed a 30 wt%
MEA solution, which will be the basis for comparing the performance
of advanced process configurations and new solvents. The Amager
Bakke WtE facility, operated by Amager Resource Center (ARC), is
expected to emit 600,000 tonnes of CO2 annually. Therefore, CCUS in-
tegration is essential to achieve net-zero emissions. To gain knowledge
and experience before constructing a full-scale facility, the Technical
University of Denmark (DTU) conducted pilot-scale tests on-site from
September to December 2021. In addition to the base case experiments,
the tests included several process configurations such as rich recycle,
split feed, inter-cooled absorber, interheated stripper, Lean Vapour
Recompression (LVC), and flexible operation of the pilot with 30 wt%
MEA. Several new solvent formulations have also been tested with
the above-mentioned process configurations. New solvent formulations
and advanced process configurations have the potential to reduce the
specific reboiler duty (SRD) considerably. To determine the extent of
optimisation obtained, it is essential to establish the operation of the
pilot in a base case mode which serves as the standard for comparison.
The current article details the performance of the pilot plant using
30 wt% MEA operated in a base case configuration. In addition, a
parametric analysis was conducted to better understand the perfor-
mance of the pilot at varying operating conditions. Literature available
on CO2 capture typically provides a very superficial analysis of the
experimental data, often limiting the discussion around the SRD. It is
crucial to obtain a thorough understanding of the several aspects of
the pilot functionality for efficient full-scale implementation of such a
capture plant. This work gives an in-depth insight into various aspects
of the pilot plant which are most often overlooked.
2. Description of the Mobile Test Unit
DTU, in collaboration with Pentair, has designed and constructed
a Mobile Test Unit (MTU) for pilot-scale CO2 capture. The pilot is de-
signed to test using aqueous MEA or similar solvents to clean 150 Nm3/h
flue gas. A 3D drawing of the pilot plant is presented in Fig. 1(a). In
addition, a picture of the pilot is presented in Fig. 1(b).
Fig. 1(a) shows that the pilot consists of four columns: absorber,
desorber, wash tower, and an additional column for advanced con-
figurations. It has two skids within which several process equipment,
such as filters, heat exchangers, pumps, flow meters, etc., are placed.
In addition to flexibility in terms of solvent use, the MTU allows the
testing of several advanced configurations which enhance absorption,
allow for heat integration, and optimise energy consumption for solvent
regeneration. A simplified Process Flow Diagram (PFD) of the pilot is
provided in Fig. 2. However, the pilot plant can have several process
configurations not presented in the PFD.
The gas and solvent flow through the system are configured for
a base case operation. The processes involved are briefed in the sub-
sequent sections. A detailed description of the pilot is available in
Appendix A.
2.1. Gas flow
Fig. 2 shows that before the flue gas enters the absorber, it goes
through a knock-out drum. The knock-out drum acts as a gas–water
separator, removing the condensate from the water-saturated flue gas.
The condensate collected in the knock-out drum is removed through
an Armstrong valve provided at the bottom of the drum. An Armstrong
valve is a gas–liquid separator that removes condensate from the gas
phase. The flue gas entering the pilot has a temperature of around
35–45 ◦C. From the knock-out drum, the flue gas enters the activated
carbon filter that is designed to remove any Hydrogen Chloride (HCl)
or Hydrogen Fluoride (HF) that is present in the flue gas as shown in

=== PDF p.4 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
4
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. 1. Depiction of the pilot plant. (a) 3D model of the pilot plant. (b) The pilot plant at the waste-power plant. Inlet (left pipe) from and exhaust (right pipe) to the power
plant.
Fig. 2. Process Flow Diagram of the pilot for base case.
Fig. 2. Accumulation of these components in the solvent could result
in corrosion of the equipment. Furthermore, if the salts formed by
MEA with HCl or HF do not efficiently decompose under stripping
conditions, they form heat-stable amine salts (HSAS). HSAS reduce
the capacity of the solvent and create operational difficulties such as
foaming [50]. Fig. 2 shows that it is possible to bypass the carbon
filter to study the influence of HCl or HF on the operation. However,
during operation, the carbon filter is always in use. Components such as
NO𝑥, O2, NO, CO, NH3, HCl, and HF were measured by the WtE facility
before the flue gas was supplied to the pilot plant. The impact of these
components on the degradation of the solvent and the accumulation of
HSAS is presented in a separate work [51].
The flue gas leaving the carbon filter is then forced into the absorber
by a side channel blower, designed to overcome the pressure drop of
the flue gas and return the clean gas at the same pressure as the inlet
gas. The flow rate of the flue gas can be adjusted by changing the
speed of the blower or by manipulating the position of the regulating
valve installed downstream of the blower. The flue gas flow rate was
maintained at 95–110 kg/h during regular operation. The flue gas
enters the absorber at the bottom and flows counter-current to the

=== PDF p.5 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
5
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. 3. PFD of the wash tower.
solvent flow. The CO2 concentration in the gas phase at the inlet and
the outlet is measured by Vaisala GMP 251 probes. The CO2 lean flue
gas leaving the absorber passes through a second column known as the
wash tower. It is also possible to bypass the wash tower and exit the
pilot, as shown in Fig. 2. The wash tower consists of three sections:
water wash, acid wash, and polishing section, detailed in the following
subsections. An illustration of the wash tower is presented in Fig. 3.
Fig. 3 shows the three sections in the wash tower. The bottom
section is used as a water wash, the middle section is used as an
acid wash, and the topmost bed is used as a polishing section. The
water wash section is required to maintain the system’s water balance,
whereas the acid wash is used for cleaning the flue gas. The polishing
section is a final cleaning step before the gas is returned to the exhaust
line.
2.1.1. Maintenance of water balance
The chemical absorption of CO2 is exothermic [52], which causes
a temperature increase in the absorber and loss of water from the
solvent. As shown in Fig. 3, the bottom of the wash tower is used
for water washing, and the cooling water flow rate is controlled by
a Proportional–Integral (PI) controller based on the absorber level.
When the pilot was tested with hot flue gas, the temperature difference
between the inlet and outlet flue gas was used as the input parameter
for controlling the cooling water flow rate. Fig. 3 also shows a level
controller in the water wash sump, which monitors the water level and
sends excess water back to the absorber. The excess water enters the
absorber through a spray nozzle above the topmost bed. Loss of water
increases the solvent concentration and requires the manual addition
of makeup water regularly in the absence of a wash tower.
2.1.2. Cleaning flue gas
As mentioned earlier, the chemical absorption of CO2 generates
heat and causes amine slip in the flue gas. Therefore, limiting amine
emissions into the atmosphere requires cleaning the flue gas before it
joins the ARC exhaust line. In addition to a water wash, an acid wash
section is available, which uses 0.1 M sulphuric acid to neutralise the
basic solvent vapours. The pH of the acid is continuously monitored
to track amine build-up. as shown in Fig. 3. After the acid wash, the
flue gas goes through a polishing step. The polishing section consists
of circulating water, the final cleaning step. The acid wash was only
used during emission measurements, while the water wash and pol-
ishing sections were constantly in use. The wash tower is detailed in
Appendix A.7.
The flue gas exiting the wash tower can either be recycled back
into the absorber or exit the pilot and join the waste-energy plant’s
exhaust stream. In the base case campaigns, the system was operated
in an open-loop configuration. Therefore, the clean flue gas joins the
exhaust gas from the waste-energy plant.
2.2. Liquid flow
As previously mentioned, a 30 wt% MEA solution was used for the
base case campaigns. During the base case operation, the lean solvent
enters the absorber at the top stage and comes in contact with the
flue gas in a counter-current direction. The absorber has four packing
sections and a total height of 19.36 m. The top packing section is
approximately 1 m tall, the second section measures 4.35 m, and the
bottom two sections measure 5.3 m. The absorber has a liquid sump at
the bottom. The bottom three packing sections are meant for absorption
of CO2, while the top section is meant to add water from the wash
tower through a spray nozzle. The solvent is regenerated in the stripper,
which has a total height of 17.54 m. The stripper consists of five
packing sections. The topmost section is 0.5 m tall and is used as a
polishing section for the stripped CO2. The following three sections are
3.5 m each, and the bottom-most section is 3.4 m tall. The rich solvent
is fed to the stripper at the top of the third packing section. Further
details about the absorber and stripper are presented in Appendix A.2
and Appendix A.3, respectively.
From Fig. 2, it can be observed that the CO2 rich solvent leaving the
bottom of the absorber is pumped to the main heat exchanger (main

=== PDF p.6 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
6
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
HE), with a maximum heating surface area of approximately 102.0 m2.
The rich solvent leaving the absorber is between 50–60 ◦C and needs
to be heated to 100–110 ◦C before entering the stripper. The main HE
accomplishes this heating process. The hot stream entering the main
HE is the lean solvent at 110–120 ◦C, leaving the bottom of the flash
vessel. The heated rich stream leaves the main HE at 100–105 ◦C, while
the cold lean stream leaves at 60–65 ◦C. The cold lean solvent leaving
the main HE enters another plate heat exchanger called the lean cooler,
with a heating surface area of 1.4 m2, meant to reduce the temperature
of the lean solvent to 40 ◦C.
3. Methodology
3.1. Operation of the pilot plant
The pilot plant consists of equipment such as the blower, pumps,
air cooler, reboiler, and motorised valves (MOV). These equipment
could be turned on/off or controlled through Digital or Analog signals
from a Programmable Logic Controller (PLC). The PLC is an interface
between the pilot plant and the HMI (Human Machine Interface). The
PLC monitors the input signals from all the connected devices. Based
on the inputs, it provides an output signal to change the state of the
device based on the programme’s logic. The signals are either Analog
I/O or Digital I/O. Digital signals are meant for switching the state of a
device between on and off modes (such as for pumps, air coolers, etc.),
whereas Analog signals are meant to control the state of the device in
a range between on/off modes (such as controlling the position of a
MOV). The process parameters, such as temperatures, flows, pressures,
etc., are sent as a 4–20 mA signal to the PLC, wherein they are
converted to a corresponding magnitude. These converted values are
available to the operator through the HMI. All the process parameters
can be viewed as time plots to monitor the pilot’s performance and
detect any operational issues. The process values are measured for
every second. The data is stored in the PLC and can be extracted as
a .csv file for a detailed analysis.
3.1.1. Start-up procedure
When starting up the pilot plant, two options were available: man-
ual mode or auto mode. If the manual mode was selected, the pumps
and reboiler had to be started up manually through the HMI, and
the valve positions for MOVs had to be provided. The rich and lean
pumps were then switched on to establish a steady solvent flow through
the absorber and stripper, followed by the reboiler which caused the
temperature in the stripper to increase. It took around 3 h for the
stripper to heat up to the desired temperature of 80–100 ◦C. After this,
the blower and water wash pump were started to begin the capture
process. On the other hand, if the auto mode was selected, a control
architecture built into the PLC ensured the start-up of the pilot in the
same order as the manual mode with several checks in place. The
plant was always run in auto mode to ensure a safe shutdown if any
process parameters crossed the safety value. Additionally, it helped in
the remote operation of the pilot.
To ensure a safe shutdown of the pilot, a trip system was designed as
part of the control structure. Most of the process parameters were pro-
vided with high and low trip values, and the trip system got triggered
if any process parameters exceeded the safety limits. In case of a plant
trip, the flue gas availability to the pilot was cut off, and the reboiler
was switched off to allow the solvent to cool down. The solvent loop
was allowed to circulate for 20 to 30 min after the reboiler was shut
off. The trip system allowed for an easy start-up of the pilot in case a
minor disturbance resulted in a plant trip, and helped in the continuous
and remote operation of the pilot.

3.1.2. Steady state operation
The plant was operated 24 h a day. Two steady states could be
obtained during the daytime, provided there were no major operational
issues. The PLC was programmed to allow for a step change of one or
more parameters after regular periods. Parameters such as solvent flow
rate, gas flow rate, and reboiler duty could be increased or decreased
in steps. The set point to the corresponding PI (Proportional–Integral)
controller was updated when a step change was implemented. The
controller consequently manipulated the MOV to change the parameter
to the new desired value. The plant could reach a new steady state with
the updated parameter for 2.5–3 h before revamping the parameter
to another value. This provided the possibility of obtaining multiple
steady states during the night and weekend operations. Fig. 4 presents
an example of one such step change operation.
From Fig. 4, it can be observed that the rich solvent flow rate was
changed in steps of 30–50 kg/h every 2.5 h. Due to the programming in-
corporated in the PLC, changing more than one parameter at a time was
possible. This allowed for conducting a parametric analysis of the whole
process. However, some of the parameters could not be adjusted during
the remote operation of the pilot, as they could be manipulated through
hand valves. Additionally, collecting samples for the steady states
obtained through remote operation was not possible. Consequently,
the densities of the rich and the lean solvent and their temperatures
are utilised for estimating the corresponding loadings. The solvent
concentration obtained through the samples collected during the day
or a few days earlier is used for the loading estimation.
3.2. Experimental campaign
A parameter study was conducted in the base case configuration
of the pilot plant. Several parameters were varied to investigate their
influence on the operational efficiency of the pilot plant. Parameters
such as the L/G ratio, CO2 capture efficiency, and F-factor were varied.
The explanation for the abbreviations is provided in the Nomenclature.
The definitions of these parameters are provided in the subsequent
sections. An overview of the parameters involved in the sensitivity
analysis conducted is presented in Table 2.
As shown in Fig. 4, each steady state obtained was maintained for
2 to 3 h and the results obtained were stable. Some of the tests were
repeated to ensure their reproducibility. However, conducting experi-
ments on a pilot-scale plant at an industrial facility has many challenges
and uncertainties. Due to the uncertainties involved, it is not feasible

=== PDF p.7 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
7
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. 4. Steady state operation with step changes in the rich solvent flow rate and reboiler duty.
to conduct multiple repetitions of the steady-state experiments. The
same process conditions for every steady state cannot be guaranteed,
and effectively, every new steady state is slightly off compared to the
previous one. Therefore, experiments involving different parameters
and configurations are continuously changed instead of repeating the
same. Additionally, the creation of more data helps in determining the
scatter in the trend of changing a process variable.
The campaigns were determined by conducting a basic mass balance
analysis to obtain the solvent and gas flow rates. Initial experiments
were conducted to test the operability of the pilot and tune the PI
controllers. Following this, campaigns were designed to analyse the
influence of different parameters on the performance of the pilot plant.
3.3. Methodology for loading determination
The samples’ loadings are obtained using a density-wt%CO2 cor-
relation, which correlates the density of a loaded solvent with the
weight fraction of CO2 in the solvent. The correlation was built by
creating samples of different known concentrations of CO2 in 30 wt%
MEA-H2O solvent. The concentration of CO2 was confirmed by BaCl2
titration method [53]. The densities of these samples were measured to
obtain the correlation between density and weight fraction of CO2. The
density measurements in the lab were conducted at 25 ◦C. However, the
densities measured in the PLC are used in the steady states for which
samples could not be collected. The solvent temperatures in the pilot
differ vastly from 25 ◦C. Therefore, the density-𝑤𝑡%CO2 correlation is
extended to include the temperature impact. Experimental data [54]
was used to build a model which could take MEA concentration,
density, and temperature as inputs and give 𝑤𝑡%CO2 as an output. The
density and wt%CO2 are linearly correlated. The correlation is of the
form:
𝜌loaded [g∕ml] = 𝜌unloaded + 𝑎⋅𝑤𝑡%CO2
(1)
where 𝜌loaded and 𝜌unloaded are the densities of the loaded and unloaded
solvents, 𝑎is the fitted parameter, and 𝑤𝑡%CO2 is the weight percentage
of CO2 in the loaded solvent. The slope 𝑎of the linear correlation
is independent of temperature. If the densities of the loaded and the
unloaded solvents are known, then the concentration of CO2 can be
easily determined. The density of the loaded rich and lean solvents
was continuously measured in the pilot plant. The difficulty arises in
estimating the density of the unloaded solvent.

3.3.1. Model for density of MEA-H2O system
A model based on Redlich–Kister expressions [55] was built for
obtaining the density of the unloaded MEA-H2O system. The model
can determine the solvent’s density by considering the concentration
of MEA and temperature as inputs. Experimental data from the liter-
ature [54] were used to build this model. The equations used are as
follows:
𝑀𝑊avg [g∕mol] = 𝑥MEA ⋅𝑀𝑊MEA + 𝑥H2O ⋅𝑀𝑊H2O
(2)
𝑉est,𝑇[ml∕mol] = 𝑥MEA ⋅𝑉MEA,𝑇+ 𝑥H2O ⋅𝑉H2O,𝑇+ 𝑉excess,𝑇
(3)
𝑉excess,𝑇[ml∕mol] = 𝑥MEA ⋅𝑥H2O ⋅(𝛴3
𝑖=0[𝑥MEA −𝑥H2O]𝑖⋅𝐴𝑖
𝑖)
(4)
𝜌unloaded,est [g∕ml] =
𝑀𝑊avg
𝑉est,𝑇
(5)
where 𝑥𝑖represents the mole fraction of component i, 𝑀𝑊𝑖is the
molecular weight of component i, 𝑉est,T and 𝑉excess,T are the estimated
molar and excess volumes of the MEA-H2O mixture at temperature
𝑇, and 𝜌unloaded,est is the estimated density of an unloaded MEA-H2O
mixture. 𝐴0, 𝐴1, 𝐴2, 𝐴3 are fitted parameters, which are functions of
temperature and are defined as follows:
𝐴𝑖[ml∕mol] = 𝐴𝑖𝑜+ 𝐴𝑖𝑇(𝑇[K] −298.15)
(6)

are presented in Table 3.
The estimated and experimental densities for different concentra-
tions of MEA at different temperatures are presented in Fig. 5(a),
and the relative difference between the estimated and experimental
densities are presented in Fig. 5(b).
From Figs. 5(a) and 5(b), it can be observed that the estimated
densities for different concentrations of MEA-H2O at different tempera-
tures are in good agreement with the experimental values. The relative
difference between the estimated and the experimental values is less
than ±0.2%. At lower temperatures, there is a slight overestimation of
the densities by the model and at high temperatures, there is a slight
underestimation of the densities.

3.3.2. Model for density of MEA-H2O -CO2 system
Solvents with know amounts of MEA-H2O-CO2 were prepared. The
loading of CO2 in these samples was determined by the BaCl2 titration

measurements were conducted at 25 ◦C. Experimental data from liter-

density of a loaded solvent of known MEA concentration and wt%CO2
is as follows:
𝜌loaded,est [g∕ml] = 𝜌unloaded,est + 𝑎⋅𝑤𝑡%CO2
(7)
𝑎[g∕ml] = 9.8924 ⋅10−3

and unloaded solvent. 𝜌unloaded,est is obtained by Eq. (5) following the
methodology described in Section 3.3.1. The estimated and experi-
mental densities for 30 wt% MEA solvent at different loadings and
temperatures, are presented in Fig. 6(a). Fig. 6(b) presents the relative
difference between these two values.

densities are in fair agreement with the experimental values with a
relative difference less than ±0.5%. The fitted parameter 𝑎applies
Fig. 6. Comparison of estimated and experimental densities for MEA-H2O-CO2 systems.
(a) Experimental densities vs. estimated densities. (b) The relative difference between
experimental and estimated densities.
to concentrations of MEA in the range 20–40 wt% even though the
estimated values presented here are limited to 30 wt% MEA.
3.3.3. Online estimation of loading for steady states
Samples from the rich and the lean streams were collected regularly
to determine the concentration of MEA in the loaded samples. The MEA
concentration of a loaded solvent, unloaded solvent, and wt%CO2 are
related to each other as follows:
𝑤𝑡%MEA,loaded [%] = 𝑤𝑡%MEA,unloaded ⋅
(100 −𝑤𝑡%𝐶𝑂2
100
)
(9)
The concentration of MEA in a loaded solvent was determined by titrat-
ing with 0.5M HCl solution. The densities and the temperatures of the
rich and the lean solvents are continuously measured. Determination
of loaded MEA concentration and thus 𝑤𝑡%CO2 converts into a system
of two equations with two unknowns:
𝜌loaded [g∕ml] = 𝜌unloaded{𝑤𝑡%MEA,unloaded} + 𝑎⋅𝑤𝑡%CO2
(10)
𝑤𝑡%MEA,unloaded [%] = 𝑤𝑡%MEA,loaded ⋅
(
100
100 −𝑤𝑡%CO2
)
(11)

Solving these two equations gives the concentration of CO2 and un-

mined as follows:
𝛼[mol CO2∕mol MEA] =
𝑤𝑡%CO2

The solvent concentration in the system was expected to be constant
throughout the day for the steady states for which samples could not
be collected. By using this method, the rich and lean loadings of the
system can be determined by minimal experimental work.
4. Results and discussion
In this section, the results obtained from the steady-state base
case experiments will be discussed in detail. The influence of varying
operating parameters, such as reboiler duty, solvent flow rate, and flue
gas flow rate, will be analysed and presented.
4.1. Comparison of methods for determination of CO2 flow rate
Before discussing the performance of the pilot at various conditions,
the validity of one of the essential performance indicators, which is CO2
captured, is verified. This validation is done by determining the CO2
captured at steady-states by three different methods and comparing
them against each other. The three methods of determination are as
follows:
1. Gas Phase: Based on the CO2 concentrations and mass flow rates
of the raw and cleaned flue gas
𝑚CO2,𝑔[kg∕h] = (𝑦CO2,in ⋅𝑛flue gas,in −𝑦CO2,out ⋅𝑛flue gas,out) ⋅𝑀𝑊CO2
(13)
Where 𝑦CO2,in and 𝑦CO2,out are inlet and outlet CO2 concentra-
tions, and 𝑛flue gas,in and 𝑛flue gas,out are inlet and outlet molar gas
flow rates, calculated based on the mass flow rates. The inlet gas
concentration and flow rate are measured after the blower, while
the outlet gas concentration and flow rate are measured after
the wash tower presented in Fig. 2. 𝑀𝑊CO2,in is the molecular
weight of CO2.
2. Liquid Phase: Based on the loadings and mass flow rates of the
lean and rich solvent
𝑚CO2,𝑙[kg∕h] = (𝛼rich ⋅𝑛rich,MEA −𝛼lean ⋅𝑛lean,MEA) ⋅𝑀𝑊CO2
(14)
where 𝛼rich and 𝛼lean are rich and lean loadings. The loadings are
estimated by the method described in Section 3.3.3. 𝑛rich,MEA and
𝑛lean,MEA are the molar flow rates of MEA in the rich and lean
solvents, respectively. The rich and lean solvent mass flow rates
are measured after the rich and lean pumps shown in Fig. 2.
3. Stripper top: Based on the CO2 flow rate obtained at the con-
denser shown in Fig. 2.
𝑚CO2,𝑠[kg∕h] = 𝑚total,𝑐−𝑚H2O,𝑐
(15)
The CO2 leaving the condenser is assumed to be saturated with
water. 𝑚total,𝑐represents the total flow rate of the stream leaving
the condenser, and 𝑚H2O,𝑐is the water content in the gas phase.
CO2 flow from the stripper top is obtained by deducting the
water content from the total measured flow.
The captured CO2 flow rate estimated by these three methods is pre-
sented in Fig. 7.
From Fig. 7, it can be observed that the degree of agreement
amongst the three different methods varies at different steady-state
points. It can be observed that the CO2 captured lies in the range of
5 to 20 kg/h. From steady-states 1–10, there are a few discrepancies
among the three methods. From steady-states 10 to 50, the estimations
done by the three methods agree. For steady-states 50 to 85, the CO2
captured determined by the gas phase and stripper top appears to
agree. However, the liquid phase estimation is consistently lower than
the other two. Several factors can influence the determination of CO2
captured by these three methods, which are briefed in the following
sections. A list of all the symbols, abbreviations, and subscripts is
provided in the nomenclature.
4.1.1. Gas phase
The CO2 captured value estimated by the gas phase depends on
the CO2 concentrations measured by the probes and the pitot gas flow
meters. The flow meters have been calibrated, and the methodology
is presented in Appendix C. However, external influences, such as the
presence of condensate, can influence the flow rate measurements.
The probes utilised for concentration measurement come with factory
calibration. Regardless, the concentration readings are also influenced
by the presence of condensate. Therefore, inaccuracies are observed in
the determined CO2 captured.
4.1.2. Liquid phase
The calculations involved in this method rely on the concentration
of the solvent, the density measurements, the temperature measure-
ments, the solvent flow rates, and the density-loading correlation.
As most of the presented steady states have been obtained during
the nighttime operations, samples could not be collected to deter-
mine solvent concentration. Therefore, the concentrations obtained

=== PDF p.10 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
10
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. 8. Comparison of determined CO2 captured by different methods. (a) CO2 captured determined through gas phase vs. liquid phase. (b) CO2 captured determined through gas
phase vs. stripper top. (c) CO2 captured determined through stripper top vs. liquid phase.
during the daytime operations are used. Solvent concentration, den-
sity measurements, mass flow rates, and loading-density model are
utilised to determine CO2 captured through the liquid phase, as detailed
in Section 3.3.3. Inaccuracies in the online measurements, density-
loading model, or lab measurements can lead to uncertainties in the
determination of liquid phase CO2 captured.
4.1.3. Stripper top
This method relies on measuring the flow of CO2 gas that is stripped
and cooled. The only estimation required is to determine the water
content in the gas, which is assumed to be saturated with water vapour.
This assumption is reasonable as the flow measurement is taken after
the CO2 exits the condenser, where it is cooled, and the water vapour
is condensed. The SITRANS FC300 flow meter is used to measure the
flow rate, density, and temperature of the CO2 leaving the condenser.
The flow meter measurement is based on the Coriolis principle. How-
ever, this flow meter is sensitive to external vibrations and two-phase
fluids in the flow stream. In addition, a zero-point standard deviation
results in a measurement error that depends on the zero point and the
measured flow rate. The comparison of the methods mentioned above
is presented in Figs. 8(a), 8(b), and 8(c).
Figs. 8(a), 8(b), and 8(c) depict the extent of agreement amongst
the three methods used for determining the CO2 captured. Fig. 8(a)
shows that the liquid phase determination is lower than the gas phase
determination. A similar trend is also observed when the liquid phase
and stripper top values are compared in Fig. 8(c). However, the gas
phase and stripper top agree with a relative difference of ±10%. As
the liquid phase determination depends on many measurements within
the pilot and the lab, it is more prone to inaccuracies. The standard
deviation and the standard error in the CO2 flow rate calculations are
presented in Fig. 9.
From Fig. 9, it can be observed that a majority of the steady states
have a standard deviation of the determined CO2 flow less than 2 kg/h
and a corresponding standard error less than 10%. However, there are a
few steady states where the standard deviation and standard error and
higher, indicating a lower accuracy. Some of the main causes of lower
accuracy are malfunctioning CO2 probes due to condensation of water
vapour from saturated gas, ill-maintained water balance due to wash
column operational difficulties, and biased Coriolis flow measurements
due to droplets of water in the gas phase.
Based on the analysis, it can be seen that each method has inherent
uncertainties, thus making it difficult to declare the true measurement
method. Therefore, the final CO2 captured value is obtained as the aver-
age of the two methods which agree the most. The pilot’s performance
under varying operating conditions will be discussed in detail in the
subsequent sections.

4.2. Stripping intensity
Stripping intensity is the ratio of energy provided to the reboiler
(reboiler duty) to the rich solvent flow rate to the stripper. It is a
parameter independent of solvent flow rates and reboiler duties. In the
experiments analysed in the current section, the stripping intensity was
varied by changing the reboiler duty at a constant solvent flow rate
and a constant L/G ratio. The expressions for determining the stripping
intensity of the total rich solvent flow and the L/G ratio are as follows:
Stripping intensity [kJ∕kgtotal] = 𝑅𝐷⋅3600
𝑚rich
(16)
𝐿∕𝐺[kg∕kg] =
𝑚lean
𝑚flue gas,in
(17)
where 𝑅𝐷is the reboiler duty [kW], 𝑚rich [kg/h] is the rich solvent flow
rate to the stripper, 𝑚lean [kg/h] is the lean solvent flow rate to the
absorber, 𝑚flue gas,in [kg/h] is the flue gas flow rate to the absorber. The
term can be made independent of the concentration of the solvent by
changing the total solvent flow rate to the MEA in the rich solvent flow
rate. The equation for obtaining the stripping intensity of the MEA flow
rate in the rich solvent to the stripper is as follows:
Stripping intensity [kJ∕kgMEA] =
𝑅𝐷⋅3600
𝑚rich ⋅𝑤𝑡MEA,loaded,rich
(18)
where 𝑤𝑡MEA,loaded,rich, is the weight fraction of MEA in the loaded rich
solvent. The stripping intensity of the MEA flow is relevant when the
solvent concentration is expected to vary widely among the considered
steady states. The MEA concentration for the steady states considered
in this section is constant at approximately 27 wt%. Therefore, the total
stripping intensity is considered the varying parameter, and its impact
on the pilot is studied. The most important process parameters for these
series of steady states are summarised in Table 4.
The specific reboiler duty (SRD) of the system at the varying strip-
ping intensities in terms of the total rich solvent flow rate [kJ/kgtotal]
and MEA flow rate in the rich solvent [kJ/kgMEA] are presented in
Figs. 10(a) and 10(b). The capture efficiency (𝜖) at the varying total
stripping intensities is shown in Fig. 10(c).
Fig. 10(a) shows that a stripping intensity of 200–210 kJ/kgtotal
is optimal for a minimum SRD of 3.65 GJ/tonne CO2. A U-shaped
curve is observed when the SRD is plotted with respect to the stripping
intensity. The increasing SRD value to the left of the optimum is caused
due to the heat loss in the main HE. To the right of the optimum, high
SRD values are observed due to excess steam production in the stripper.
These phenomena are discussed in further detail in this section. A
similar U-shaped curve is observed in Fig. 10(b). The stripping intensity
with respect to the MEA flow rate in the rich solvent flow erases the
dependence on the concentration of the solvent. As the rich solvent has
a constant MEA concentration of 30.4 wt%, the shape of the U-curve
is preserved. Therefore, utilisation of MEA-based stripping intensity is
redundant when the solvent concentration is unchanged.
The corresponding capture efficiencies (𝜖) can be noted in Fig. 10(c).
The SRD and 𝜖of the pilot are determined as follows:
SRD [GJ∕tonne CO2] = 𝑅𝐷⋅3.6
𝑚CO2
(19)
𝜖[%] =
𝑦CO2,in ⋅𝑛flue gas,in −𝑦CO2,out ⋅𝑛flue gas,out
𝑦CO2,in𝑛flue gas,in
⋅100
(20)
With increasing stripping intensities, 𝜖achieved in the absorber
increases. 𝜖also depends on the CO2 concentration in the flue gas
and the flow rate. The concentration and the flow rate are constant
at 12.5 vol% and 100 kg/h as mentioned in Table 4. At stripping
intensities higher than 230 kJ/kgtotal, capture efficiencies between 98
to 100% were obtained. To achieve 𝜖of 90%, around 200 kJ/kgtotal of
stripping intensity is required. This stripping intensity corresponds to
an SRD of approximately 3.7 ± 0.1 GJ/tonne CO2. Therefore, for an L/G
ratio of 2.75 kg/kg, operating the pilot in the stripping intensity range
of 200–210 kJ/kgtotal is ideal. The stripping intensity impacts the SRD
as it changes the lean loading of the solvent. Consequently, 𝜖is also
affected. The solvent is not lean enough at low stripping intensities,
leading to low 𝜖. However, as the stripping intensity increases, the lean
loading of the solvent is expected to decrease and cause a loss of energy
as steam from the stripper top. A low lean loading causes an increase
in the cyclic capacity of the solvent and 𝜖. The solvent cyclic capacity
(𝛥𝛼) is the difference between the rich and lean loadings of the solvent.
𝛥𝛼[mol CO2∕mol MEA] = 𝛼rich −𝛼lean
(21)
The effect of stripping intensity on the lean loading of the solvent and
the reboiler temperature is presented in Figs. 11(a) and 12(a).
Fig. 11(a) indicates that the lean loading decreases as the stripping
intensity increases. At higher stripping intensity, the energy available
for the desorption of CO2 increases, causing a decrease in the lean load-
ing as displayed in Fig. 11(b). At higher stripping intensities, the duty
spent on desorption of CO2 is more prominent, causing the solvent to be
leaner. The influence of stripping intensity on the reboiler temperature
and the steam pressure is presented in Fig. 12(a). Additionally, the
stripper pressures maintained in the column at the bottom and the top
are presented in Fig. 12(b).
Figs. 12(a) and 12(b) indicate that the steam pressure influences
the reboiler temperature and the stripper pressure at the bottom. The
pressure in the stripper is controlled by a PI controller at the top.
However, Fig. 12(b) shows that the correlation between the stripper
top and bottom pressure is not as strong as that between the stripper
bottom pressure and the steam pressure. This phenomenon can be
understood by analysing the functioning of the reboiler. The following
equation represents the transfer of energy in the reboiler
𝑅𝐷[kW] = 𝑈⋅𝐴𝑟⋅𝛥𝑇𝐿𝑀𝑇𝐷
(22)
As the duty provided to the reboiler changes, the only two variables
that could change to accommodate the changed duty are the overall

=== PDF p.12 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
12
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. 10. SRD and 𝜖as a function of stripping intensity at L/G = 2.75 kg/kg. (a) Impact of the total stripping intensity on the SRD of the system. The dotted lines represent a
trendline of the data. (b) Impact of the MEA stripping intensity on the SRD of the system. (c) Impact of the total stripping intensity on 𝜖.
heat transfer coefficient (𝑈) and the temperature difference (𝛥𝑇𝐿𝑀𝑇𝐷).
The area (𝐴𝑟) of the reboiler in the current set-up is invariable. Addi-
tionally, the value of 𝑈is expected to stay the same. Consequently, the
reboiler’s temperature increases when the duty provided increases. The
energy increase is expected to be converted to higher steam pressure,
causing an increase in the temperature.
With varying stripping intensity, the SRD was observed to change
in Fig. 10(a). The SRD could be influenced by the performance of
the main HE and the energy lost in the stripper top as water vapour.
Logarithmic Mean Temperature Difference (LMTD) can indicate the
performance and heat loss of the main HE. The performance of the main
HE consequently impacts the duty spent on the sensible heating of the
solvent in the stripper. The LMTD of the main HE and the duty spent on
sensible heating are presented in Fig. 13(a). Additionally, the condenser
duty and steam lost at the stripper top with changing stripping intensity
are presented in Fig. 13(b).
Fig. 13(a) displays that the duty spent for sensible heating of the
solvent depends more on the efficiency of the main HE than the
stripping intensity. The efficiency of the main HE is evaluated based
on the LMTD value. A low LMTD value indicates that the temperature
differences on the hot and cold sides are similar, implying a sound
performance of the heat exchanger. As the stripping intensity increases
from 170 to 210 kJ/kgtotal, the LMTD appears to fluctuate around 12 ◦C.
Beyond a stripping intensity of 210 kJ/kgtotal, the LMTD of the main
HE visibly decreases, indicating better efficiency. Consequently, the
duty spent on sensible heating of the solvent entering the stripper also
reduces. When higher duties are spent on the sensible heating of the
solvent, the duty available for desorbing CO2 would be lower, leading
to higher SRD values.
Fig. 13(b) shows the influence of stripping intensity on the con-
denser duty and the amount of steam lost at the stripper top. To better
explain the method of determination of the water vapour and duty lost
as water vapour, an illustration of the PFD of the stripper section is
presented in Fig. 14.
Fig. 14 shows that the temperature and pressure of the streams
exiting the stripper top (stream 1) and gas–liquid separator top (stream
3) are measured. Additionally, the mass flow rate of stream 3 is also
measured. Assuming that stream 3 is saturated with water vapour, the
moles of CO2 and water can be determined. Based on the pressure and
temperature measurements of stream 1, along with the assumption of
saturation, the water content in stream 1 can be calculated as follows:
𝑛H2O,1 [mol∕h] =
𝑛H2O,3
𝑦𝑠𝑎𝑡1
(23)
where 𝑛H2O,1 and 𝑛H2O,3 are the number of moles of water in streams 1
and 3 respectively and 𝑦𝑠𝑎𝑡1 is the saturated pressure of water vapour
in stream 1 estimated by Antoine’s equation [56,57]. Stream 1 leaving
the stripper top is in the temperature range 80 to 100 ◦C. Higher
temperatures and water vapour loss at the stripper top lead to energy
loss. The energy lost from the stripper top is a sum of the sensible heat

3600
(27)
where 𝑅𝐷cond corresponds to the total duty lost at the stripper top.
𝑅𝐷steam, 𝑅𝐷H2O, and 𝑅𝐷CO2 refer to the duties lost through steam,
condensed water, and CO2. 𝑇𝑠𝑎𝑡is the saturation temperature cor-
responding to the pressure of stream 1. 𝜆𝑣𝑎𝑝is the latent heat of
vaporisation of water [58]. Production of steam is essential as it acts
as the stripping agent. However, excessive steam leads to energy loss
as the water vapour escapes from the stripper top only to be cooled,
condensed and added back to the solvent. An excess of 3.8 kW of
the reboiler duty and an equivalent of 3.6 kg/h of steam is lost when
the stripping intensity increases from 175 to 260 kJ/kgtotal. Therefore,
operating at high stripping intensities leads to low lean loadings, as
observed in Fig. 11(a), resulting in the loss of steam and energy at the
Fig. 12. Effect of stripping intensity (a) on reboiler temperature (b) on steam pressure
and stripper pressure.
stripper top. This energy loss causes an increase in the SRD at higher
stripping intensities, as seen in Fig. 10(a). Consequently, high capture
efficiencies observed in Fig. 10(c) are obtained at a price of high SRD.
The stripping intensity is also likely to influence the temperature profile
in the desorber, which is presented in Fig. 15.
In the desorber, several phenomena co-occur, influencing the tem-
perature. The major contributing factors are evaporation, condensation,
sensible heat transfer, and desorption of CO2. Water evaporation and
desorption of CO2 are endothermic, whereas condensation is exother-
mic [59,60]. Fig. 15 shows the influence of stripping intensity on
the temperature profile in the desorber. Three stripping intensities
are chosen to cover the range presented in the preceding figures.
The temperature profiles for the cases with stripping intensities 175
and 209 kJ/kgtotal appear similar. At these stripping intensities, the
temperatures along the length of the column from the bottom to
3.4 m decrease from approximately 118 ◦C to 98 ◦C. This temperature
reduction is likely a consequence of desorption of CO2, evaporation
of water from the solvent, and sensible heating of the solvent. From
3.4 m to 6.9 m, there is a slight increase in the temperature, possibly
due to the condensation of water vapour. From 6.9 m to 10.4 m, there
is a decrease in the temperature likely caused by the sensible heating of
the solvent flowing in the counter-current direction. In contrast, for the
case of stripping intensity = 255 kJ/kgtotal, the temperature decreases
from 120 ◦C to 110 ◦C from the bottom of the column to a height of

=== PDF p.14 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
14
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. 13. Contributions of LMTD, sensible heating, and heat lost to the SRD. (a) LMTD
and duty spent on sensible heating at increasing stripping intensity. (b) Duty and steam
lost at the stripper top at increasing stripping intensity.
6.4 m. These temperatures indicate that a larger part of the stripper is
utilised for desorption of CO2 along with the evaporation of water. The
presence of elevated temperatures is also reflected in the higher loss of
water vapour at the stripper top in Fig. 13(b) and leaner loading of the
solvent in Fig. 11(a). From 6.4 m to 10.4 m, a temperature fall suggests
the transfer of sensible heat and condensation of water vapour in the
top part of the column.
As the lean loading and stripping intensity are interrelated, the lean
loading impacts the SRD of the system as displayed in Fig. 16.
Fig. 16 displays a profile similar to Fig. 10(a). At low lean loadings,
the SRD is high due to the requirement of a high stripping intensity
and loss of heat through water vapour. Fig. 13(a) demonstrates that the
main HE performance and high sensible heat requirements result in a
slightly elevated SRD for high lean loadings. An optimal lean loading
can be observed at 0.2 mol CO2/mol MEA, where the SRD is minimum
without compromising 𝜖needs. Lean loading can also influence the
performance of the absorber. This is presented in terms of rich and lean
loadings and temperature profiles in Figs. 17(a) and 17(b).
Fig. 17(a) shows that the rich loading of the solvent remains rea-
sonably constant regardless of the lean loading. Therefore, the cyclic
capacity of the solvent increases with decreasing lean loading of the
solvent. The increased cyclic capacity is also reflected in 𝜖, as shown
in Fig. 10(c). Fig. 17(b) shows the temperature profile in the absorber

at three different lean loadings of the solvent. Even though the L/G
ratio was maintained at a constant value, it is evident that the lean
loading plays a role in the absorber temperature profile. At low lean
loadings, more MEA can absorb CO2. Additionally, the heat of absorp-
tion of CO2 in MEA is higher for loadings < 0.5 mol CO2/mol MEA.
At loadings < 0.5 mol CO2/mol MEA, the absorption of CO2 results
in the formation of carbamates. At loadings > 0.5 mol CO2/mol MEA,
physical absorption of CO2 occurs, which is not as exothermic, resulting
in lower temperature increase [61]. Consequently, higher temperatures
in the absorber are observed for lower loadings. High temperatures in
the absorber provide scope for implementing configurations such as
inter-cooling.
Based on the analysis of varying stripping intensity, it can be
concluded that operating the pilot at a total stripping intensity of 200–
220 kJ/kgtotal yields optimum performance. At this stripping intensity,
𝜖of 90%–95% can be obtained with an SRD of 3.65 to 3.8 GJ/tonne
CO2. At stripping intensities lower than this, energy is lost in the main
HE and at higher stripping intensities, energy is lost at the stripper top.
4.3. L/G ratio
The L/G ratio is the lean solvent flow rate ratio to the flue gas
flow rate. Analysing the influence of the L/G ratio helps assess the
absorber’s fluid dynamics. One of the primary outcomes of varying the
L/G ratio is the alteration in the temperature profile and the rate of
CO2 transfer from the gas phase to the liquid phase. The following
subsections provide a detailed discussion of these effects.
4.3.1. Absorber loading profiles
Three steady states with varying L/G values are being considered
to discuss their influence on the temperature and loading profiles.
The main operating parameters of these steady states are presented in
Table 5.
Liquid samples were collected along the length of the absorber for
the aforementioned steady states. The loading profiles for these states
are presented in Fig. 18(a). In addition, a mass balance analysis was
conducted based on the loading profile to determine the extent of CO2
capture along the column length, as depicted in Fig. 18(b).
Figs. 18(a) and 18(b) show that the L/G ratio impacts the packing
height required to maintain a consistent 𝜖. In the case of L/G =
2.4 kg/kg, most of the capture occurs in the absorber’s top bed. The
loading in the top bed goes from 0.2 to 0.4 mol CO2/mol MEA, account-
ing for approximately 70% of the total capture. The remaining 30%
occurs in the second bed, while the last packing bed shows negligible
capture. Thus, the primary capture occurs at the top section of the
absorber. For L/G = 2.9 kg/kg, both the top two beds of the absorber
are equally utilised for capture. In the top bed, solvent loading increases
from 0.3 to 0.4 mol CO2/mol MEA, representing 50%. In contrast,
solvent loading increases marginally in the top two beds for L/G =
3 kg/kg, accounting for around 10% of the total capture. However,

=== PDF p.15 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
15
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. 14. PFD of the stripper section.
Fig. 15. Effect of stripping intensity on the temperature profile in the stripper.
in the bottom-most bed of the absorber, the solvent loading increases
from 0.32 to 0.5 mol CO2/mol MEA, representing nearly 90% of the
total capture. In this case, the absorption of CO2 shifts to the bottom
of the absorber. Based on this analysis, the column height required to
achieve similar capture efficiencies can be determined by considering
the maintained L/G ratio. Although the L/G ratios of 2.9 and 3 kg/kg
are closely similar, a noticeable disparity in their loading profiles can
be observed. This difference is likely because the temperature within
the absorber influences the rate of CO2 transfer from the gas phase to
the liquid phase.
4.3.2. Absorber temperature profiles
The temperature profiles along the length of the absorber are de-
picted in Fig. 19(a).
Fig. 19(a) shows that the temperature profile along the absorber
is qualitatively similar for all three L/G ratios, displaying a bulge at
the top of the column. According to Fig. 18(a), one would expect a
temperature bulge at the top for L/G = 2.4 kg/kg and at the bottom
Fig. 16. Effect of lean loading on the SRD.
for L/G = 3 kg/kg, as the majority of the capture occurs at the top
and bottom, respectively, in these cases. However, temperature bulges
are consistently observed at the top for all ratios. The temperature
variations along the absorber length depend on several factors:
1. The exothermic heat of CO2 absorption.
2. The endothermic heat of water vaporisation from the solvent.
3. The exothermic heat of water condensation from the gas phase.
4. Sensible heat transfer between the phases based on temperature
differences.
The temperatures of the solvent and gas in contact at the bottom of
the absorber are presented in Fig. 19(c). From Fig. 19(c), it can be
observed that for L/G = 2.9 and 3 kg/kg, the gas entering the absorber
is more than 10 ◦C colder than the solvent it comes in contact with.
This temperature difference causes the solvent to lose heat as sensible
heat to the gas phase. Additionally, water is lost from the solvent as
the gas temperature increases, resulting in the cooling of the solvent at
the bottom of the column due to the heat of vaporisation. At the top

=== PDF p.16 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
16
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. 17. Effect of stripping intensity on the cyclic capacity of the absorber and
temperature profiles. (a) The rich and lean loadings at varying stripping intensities.
(b) Temperature profiles at different lean loadings. These loadings correspond to the
stripping intensities mentioned in Fig. 15.
of the column, the gas temperature exiting the absorber is higher than
that of the lean solvent entering the absorber. Apart from the heat of
CO2 absorption, the sensible heat exchanged between the phases and
the heat of water condensation from the gas phase contributes to the
temperature bulge at the top of the column. Due to the temperature
differences between the gas and liquid phases at the bottom of the
absorber, the temperature profiles exhibit similarities regardless of the
L/G ratio.
However, upon closer examination, it becomes apparent that the
rate of temperature increase varies with L/G. In the case of L/G =
2.4 kg/kg, there is a gradual temperature increase at the bottom. The
temperature rises from 40 to 60 ◦C as the absorber extends up to
10 m. Between 10 and 15 m, there is an approximate 15 ◦C temperature
increase. Conversely, for L/G = 3 kg/kg, a higher rate of temperature
increase is observed in the lower part of the absorber, while the temper-
ature rise becomes more gradual towards the top. These observations
align with the expected behaviour for low (< 2.5 kg/kg) and high
(> 3 kg/kg) L/G ratios, as illustrated in Fig. 19(b).
Fig. 18. CO2 concentration profiles in the absorber. (a) Loading profiles in the absorber
for different L/G ratios. (b) Capture profiles in the absorber for different L/G ratios.
4.3.3. Stripper temperature profiles
The temperature profiles in the stripper for the three L/G cases
under consideration are presented in Fig. 20.
Fig. 20 represents the temperature profiles in the stripper for the
three different cases of L/G ratio. The temperature profiles for the case
of L/G = 2.9, and 3 kg/kg are similar. The temperature in the last bed
increases from approximately 95 ◦C to 120 ◦C. The temperature in the
second bed for these cases increases slightly and then decreases in the
topmost bed of the column. Three different phenomena occur in the
stripper:
1. Generation of steam by water vaporisation
2. Desorption of CO2 from the solvent
3. Condensation of water vapour from the vapour stream
At the bottom of the stripper, where the reboiler is located, steam is
generated from the solvent. As desorption of CO2 is an endothermic
process, a decrease in temperature would be expected where it oc-
curs. For L/G = 3 kg/kg, the temperature decreases from 0 to 3.4 m.
Therefore, the bottom bed is likely utilised for desorption of CO2.
This temperature decrease can result in the condensation of water
vapour from the vapour phase, which is an exothermic process. This

=== PDF p.17 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
17
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. 19. Temperatures in the absorber. (a) Temperature profile in the absorber for
varying L/G values. (b) Characteristic curves of expected temperature profiles in the
absorber for varying L/G values. (c) Inlet and exit temperatures of the gas and liquid
phase in the absorber.
results in a slight temperature increase in the middle packing bed. As
the hot-rich solvent enters the stripper at the topmost bed, a small
temperature increase can again be observed. This behaviour differs
Fig. 20. Stripper temperature profiles at varying L/G values.
from the L/G profile 2.4 kg/kg. For the low L/G case, the solvent
flow rate used is low. To maintain a similar 𝜖, the solvent needs to
be leaner. Therefore, two packed beds are utilised for desorption of
CO2, as observed through the temperature profiles. In the bottom and
the middle beds, the temperature decreases consistently, indicating that
both these beds are utilised for desorption of CO2. In the topmost
packing bed, there is a steep decrease in temperature. This decrease
is due to the temperature difference between the hot solvent entering
the stripper and the temperature inside the stripper. The topmost bed is
used for increasing the solvent’s temperature and condensing the water
from the vapour phase. Based on the temperature profiles, a single
packing bed is sufficient for desorbing CO2 for the high L/G cases,
whereas two packing beds are sufficient for the low L/G case.
4.3.4. 𝐿𝑀𝐸𝐴∕𝐺𝐶𝑂2 ratio
As seen in the preceding section, analysing the influence of the L/G
ratio aids in assessing the fluid dynamics of the absorber and desorber.
However, the primary species involved in the gas–liquid interaction are
the MEA in the lean solvent and CO2 in the flue gas. Consequently,
the term can be further rendered independent by transforming it to the
ratio of the MEA flow rate in the lean solvent to the CO2 flow rate in
the flue gas.
𝐿MEA
𝐺CO2
[kg∕kg] =
𝑤𝑡MEA,loaded,lean ⋅𝑚lean
𝑦CO2,in ⋅𝑛flue gas,in ⋅𝑀𝑊CO2
(28)
where 𝑤𝑡lean,loaded,MEA is the weight fraction of MEA in the loaded
lean solvent, 𝑚lean is the lean solvent flow rate, 𝑦CO2,in is the inlet
gas concentration, 𝑛flue gas,in is the molar flow rate of the flue gas.
𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 is specific to the MEA and CO2 flows in the liquid and gas
phases respectively, whereas L/G accounts for the total solvent and gas
flow rates.
The 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 ratio was varied while other parameters were
constant. By adjusting the reboiler duty, 𝜖of approximately 85% was
achieved. Table 6 summarises the critical operating parameters.
As the 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 ratio varies, the amount of MEA available for
absorbing CO2 also varies. Therefore, to maintain a constant 𝜖, the
lean loading of the solvent is manipulated by changing the reboiler
duty, which affects the SRD. The SRD values corresponding to different
𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 ratios are illustrated in Fig. 21.
From Fig. 21, it is evident that the SRD vs 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 plot follows
a U-shaped curve, indicating the presence of an optimal 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2
ratio where the SRD is minimised. This optimum ratio will likely fall
within the 4 to 5.5 kg/kg range. Below and above this optimal ratio,
the SRD tends to be higher. Several factors contribute to the SRD of

S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.

Fig. 21. Influence of the 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 ratio on the SRD.
the system. The energy supplied to the reboiler is utilised for various
processes, including sensible heating, desorption of CO2, and steam
generation. The energy required for desorbing CO2 equals the energy
released during the absorption process [52]. It is important to note that
the absorption of CO2 in MEA is an exothermic process, necessitating
energy for desorption.
𝑅𝐷[kW] = 𝑅𝐷sens + 𝑅𝐷des,CO2 + 𝑅𝐷steam
(29)
where 𝑅𝐷sens is the duty spent on sensible heating of the solvent that
enters the stripper to the reboiler temperature, 𝑅𝐷des,CO2 is the duty
spent on desorption of CO2, and 𝑅𝐷steam is the duty spent on the
generation of steam. The allocation of the reboiler duty among different
energy requirements plays a significant role in determining the SRD of
the system. With variations in the reboiler duty, the stripping intensity
applied to the solvent and the MEA within the solvent is anticipated to
change. Fig. 22 illustrates the stripping intensities necessary to obtain
a consistent 𝜖across different 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 ratios.
Fig. 22 presents the stripping intensity required for both the total
solvent and the MEA in the solvent to achieve a consistent 𝜖across dif-
ferent 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 ratios. It can be observed that the stripping intensity
for the total solvent is consistently around 200 kJ/kg for 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2
values 4 to 5 kg/kg. However, for a 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 value of 3.5, the
total stripping intensity required is higher than 200 kJ/kgtotal by ap-
proximately 30%. When the stripping intensity of circulating MEA is
considered, a slightly different trend is observed. For 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values
5 to 6 kg/kg, stripping intensities around 600 kJ/kgMEA are required.
However, at lower 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values, much higher stripping intensities
were required. This is possibly due to the dilution of the solvent, which
reduced the MEA flows, leading to high stripping intensities. Stripping
intensity directly affects the loading of the lean solvent. Fig. 23 presents
the obtained lean loadings at various stripping intensities.
Fig. 22. Stripping intensities required at different 𝐿𝑀𝐸𝐴∕𝐺𝐶𝑂2 ratios to obtain 𝜖of
85%.
Fig. 23. Influence of stripping intensity of MEA on the lean loading.
From Fig. 23, it is apparent that the lean loading obtained decreases
as the stripping intensity of MEA increases. At high stripping intensities,
more energy is available for desorption of CO2, leading to low lean
loadings. At low 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 ratios, the solvent requires a low lean
loading to compensate for the lower MEA flow rates. The solvent does
not need to be very lean at high 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 ratios and therefore
requires low stripping intensities. Similarly, The 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 ratio has
an impact on the cyclic capacity of the solvent, as shown in Fig. 24.
Fig. 24 illustrates the rich and lean loadings of the solvent at
different 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 ratios. The difference between the rich and lean
loadings indicates the cyclic capacity of the solvent. It is apparent from
Fig. 24 that the rich loading decreases while the lean loading increases
with higher 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values. The gas phase’s CO2 content remains
relatively constant. Thus the total active MEA required to capture the
same amount of CO2 remains consistent. Therefore, the lean loading
of the solvent rises with increasing 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values. Additionally,
the rich loading decreases as the solvent is less saturated with CO2 at
higher 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values. In contrast, for low 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values, a
low lean loading is necessary, resulting in higher stripping intensities as
observed in Fig. 23. These high stripping intensities lead to significant
steam generation in the reboiler for achieving the desired lean loadings.
Consequently, there is energy loss in the form of unused steam at the

=== PDF p.19 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
19
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. 24. Cyclic capacity of the solvent at varying 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 ratios to obtain 𝜖of 85%.
Fig. 25. Steam and the percentage of reboiler duty lost at the stripper top with varying
𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values.
top of the stripper. In addition to the loss of steam from the stripper
top, high stripping intensities also contribute to the degradation of the
solvent [62,63]. The amount of steam lost at the top of the stripper and
the percentage of reboiler duty lost at different 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values are
presented in Fig. 25.
From Fig. 25, it can be observed that the quantity of steam lost at
the top of the stripper diminishes as the 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values increase.
This reduction is attributed to the lower stripping intensities required
for higher 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values. A smaller amount of steam is generated
at lower stripping intensities, resulting in a decreased steam loss at
the top of the stripper. The percentage of reboiler duty lost due to the
escaping steam also follows a similar trend as the quantity of steam lost.
Therefore, operating at higher 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values minimises energy loss
at the top of the stripper.
High stripping intensities, which correspond to low lean loadings,
can account for the high SRD values observed at low 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values
in Fig. 21. On the other hand, for 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values higher than
the optimum, the energy consumption for CO2 desorption decreases
due to the decreased cyclic capacity of the solvent. However, to fully
understand the reasons behind the high SRD values, it is necessary to
analyse the energy spent on sensible solvent heating. This is because
the performance of the main HE also affects the sensible heating of
Fig. 26. Performance of the main HE and the percentage of the reboiler duty excluding
the heat lost to the environment at varying 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values.
the solvent. Hence, Fig. 26 presents the performance of the main HE
and the percentage of the reboiler duty (excluding heat loss to the
environment) dedicated to sensible heating of the solvent at different
𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values.
Fig. 26 illustrates the performance of the main HE as a function of
LMTD. The performance of the main HE appears to be independent of
the 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values considered, fluctuating in the range of 10–12 ◦C.
However, the relative reboiler duty spent on the sensible heating of the
solvent increases with higher 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values. At low 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2
values, the relative duty spent on sensible heating appears unaffected
by the heat lost at the main HE. In contrast, at high 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values,
the relative duty spent closely follows the performance of the main HE.
The heat lost in the system is primarily attributed to steam loss from
the stripper top due to high stripping intensities at low 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2
values. In contrast, at high 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values, it is mainly attributed
to the performance of the main HE. An optimum point of operation
is achieved where these two phenomena converge between 4 and
5.5 kg/kg of 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2.
4.4. Influence of F-factor
The F-factor is an indicator of the fluid dynamic load in the ab-
sorber. The F-factor is dependent on the superficial gas velocity and
the gas density. The F-factor is determined as follows:
𝐹−𝑓𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑜𝑟[Pa0.5] = 𝑉⋅(𝜌flue gas)0.5
(30)
where 𝑉is the superficial gas velocity across the absorber cross-section
and 𝜌flue gas is the density of the flue gas. The influence of two F-
factors on various aspects of the pilot at different capture efficiencies is
analysed. An analysis of the influence of varying F-factors at a constant
𝜖follows this.
4.4.1. Varying 𝜖
Experiments were conducted with a constant L/G ratio of 2.7 ± 0.1
kg/kg while varying the gas and solvent flow rates. The reboiler duty
was adjusted for each selected F-factor to achieve different capture
efficiencies. Table 7 presents the key operating parameters.
The SRD of the system with respect to the stripping intensity used
for two values of F-factor is presented in Fig. 27.
Fig. 27 depicts the relationship between SRD and varying stripping
intensities for two different F-factors: 0.72 and 0.79 Pa0.5. It is evident
that the SRD values for the F-factor of 0.72 Pa0.5 consistently surpass
those for 0.79 Pa0.5 at different stripping intensities. Although the
difference between the two F-factors is not substantial, the distinction

in SRD is noticeable. Implementing higher F-factor values was limited
due to water loss through evaporation into the flue gas. To maintain the
water balance in the system, a spray nozzle at the top of the water was
intended to add water from the wash column. However, the undersized
spray nozzle resulted in water loss at higher gas flow rates. The lower
SRD at a higher F-factor can be further comprehended by examining
the rich and lean loadings in Figs. 28(a) and 28(b).
Figs. 28(a) and 28(b) show that the rich and lean loadings for
the F-factor of 0.79 Pa0.5 are higher than those for 0.72 Pa0.5. Higher
rich and lean loadings in the case of 0.79 Pa0.5 indicate that lower
energy is expended for desorption of CO2. To compensate for the
increased sensible energy demands for higher solvent flow rates, the
energy contributed to stripping CO2 is expected to have decreased.
As a result, higher lean loadings are achieved. However, the absorber
was oversized for the current operating flow ranges, leading to the
solvent attaining rich loadings exceeding 0.5 mol CO2/mol MEA, thus
maintaining the cyclic capacity of the solvent. Additionally, at higher
fluid dynamic loadings, the wetting area of the column would also
be more prominent, thereby aiding in the improved mass transfer of
CO2 between the phases. The cyclic capacity of the solvent and the
capture efficiencies obtained for the two F-factors at varying stripping
intensities are presented in Figs. 29(a) and 29(b).
The results depicted in Fig. 29(a) reveal that the cyclic capacity of
the solvent is higher for the F-factor of 0.79 Pa0.5 compared to that of
0.72 Pa0.5. These findings validate that the solvent’s ability to absorb
CO2 remains intact even at slightly elevated lean loadings due to the
oversized column. The corresponding capture efficiencies illustrated in
Fig. 29(b) exhibit similar efficiencies for both F-factor cases at varying
stripping intensities. However, for stripping intensities ranging from
200 to 220 kJ/kgtotal, the capture efficiencies for F-factor = 0.79 Pa0.5
are lower than those for F-factor = 0.72 Pa0.5. This difference can
Fig. 28. Loadings with varying stripping intensity at two different F-factors. (a) Lean
loading vs. stripping intensity. (b) Rich loading vs. stripping intensity.
be attributed to either the solvent flow rate not being sufficiently
high or the lean loading not being adequately low. Nevertheless, 𝜖of
90%, which is of interest, can be attained at a stripping intensity of
approximately 200 kJ/kgtotal for both F-factor cases.
The contribution of sensible heating to the SRD and the performance
of the main HE in terms of LMTD are presented in Figs. 30(a) and 30(b),
respectively.
From Fig. 30(a), it is evident that the proportion of sensible heating
contributing to the SRD diminishes as the stripping intensity increases,
regardless of the F-factor. However, it can be observed that the sensible
heating requirements are higher for F-factor = 0.79 Pa0.5 than for 0.72
Pa0.5. This aligns with expectations as the circulation of the solvent is
higher at larger F-factors, leading to more heat loss. Fig. 30(b) show-
cases the improved performance of the main HE at higher intensities,
resulting in a decrease in the energy consumed for sensible heating of
the solvent in the stripper. Following Fig. 30(a), it can be seen that the
performance of the heat exchanger is poorer for the higher F-factor in
the stripping intensity range of 220–260 kJ/kgtotal. The contributions
of CO2 desorption and energy loss as water vapour are illustrated in
Fig. 31(a) and Fig. 31(b), respectively.
Fig. 31(a) indicates that the contribution of CO2 desorption to
the SRD is lower for the case of F-factor = 0.79 Pa0.5. As shown in

=== PDF p.21 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
21
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. 29. Influence of stripping intensity on capture of CO2. (a) Cyclic capacity vs.
stripping intensity at two different F-factors. (b) 𝜖vs. stripping intensity at two different
F-factors.
Figs. 28(b) and 28(a), the rich and lean loadings of the solvent are
higher for the case of 0.79 Pa0.5. A higher lean loading implies lower
energy utilisation in the stripper. Additionally, a rich loading higher
than 0.5 mol CO2/mol MEA implies lower energy requirements for
CO2 desorption, thanks to the physical absorption of CO2 at loadings
> 0.5 mol CO2/mol MEA. These two factors contribute to reducing the
total desorption energy in the stripper. Fig. 31(b) shows that the energy
lost in the condenser at the stripper top increases with the stripping
intensity, which aligns with expectations. However, the energy lost in
the case of F-factor = 0.79 Pa0.5 is lower. This indicates that better
energy performance can be achieved if the plant operates at an F-
factor of 0.79 Pa0.5 while maintaining lean loadings slightly above
0.2 mol CO2/mol MEA. This approach would not compromise 𝜖while
simultaneously reducing the SRD of the system by 5%.
This analysis shows that operating the pilot at a higher F-factor than
0.72 Pa0.5 is beneficial. However, the upper limit of operation is not
apparent in terms of the SRD of the system. This will be dealt with in
the following section.

< 0.75 Pa0.5. At an F-factor of 0.62 Pa0.5, the SRD is as high as 5

GJ/tonne CO2. An optimum is obtained when the F-factor is maintained

system’s behaviour can be better understood by analysing the lean and
rich loadings required to maintain a constant 𝜖at varying F-factors.
Fig. 33 presents the lean and rich loadings.
Fig. 33 displays the rich and lean loadings at various F-factors to
obtain a constant 𝜖of 89 ± 1%. It can be observed that lean loading
required at F-factor = 0.62 Pa0.5 is almost 0.1 mol CO2/mol MEA. At
low F-factors, the wetting area available for transfer of CO2 reduces.
This results in a lower rich loading. Therefore, the solvent requires a
lower lean loading to maintain 𝜖. As shown in the preceding sections,
low lean loadings are obtained at high stripping intensities, which
causes energy loss in the form of steam from the stripper top. This
leads to a high SRD value at low F-factors. At F-factors higher than
0.79 Pa0.5, sensible heating requirements of the solvent increase, as
shown in Fig. 30(a).
Therefore, the optimal point of operation of the pilot is at an F-
factor of 0.79 Pa0.5 where the cyclic capacity of the solvent is at 0.3 mol
CO2/mol MEA and an SRD of 3.46 GJ/tonne CO2 is obtained.
In the current work, SRD values in the range 3.46 to 4.8 GJ/tonne
CO2 are obtained for all the tests conducted. The lean loadings are
observed in the range 0.12 to 0.25 mol CO2/mol MEA. The solvent
concentration was maintained in the range of 26 to 27 wt% of MEA.
For 𝜖of 90%, the minimum SRD obtained is 3.46 GJ/tonne CO2 at
an F-factor of 0.78 Pa0.5. A one-to-one comparison with pilot-scale
results is difficult due to the variations in the scale of the equipment,
flue gas conditions, and operating conditions, such as the solvent flow
rate, stripper pressure, and 𝜖. A minimum SRD of 3.6 GJ/tonne CO2
was observed for 90% 𝜖in the MEA testing done as a part of the
CASTOR project [10]. On average, an SRD of 3.7 GJ/tonnes CO2 was
observed for MEA-based CO2 capture [10]. In another study, tests
were conducted in different ranges of lean and rich loadings due to
insufficient packing height [28,64]. The experiments simulated lean
loadings in the range of 0.18 to 0.40 mol CO2/mol MEA, rich loadings
in the range of 0.26 to 0.45 mol CO2/mol MEA and SRD values in
the range of 3.7 to 11.2 GJ/tonne CO2. An optimum lean loading was
observed to be in the range of 0.23 to 0.27 mol CO2/mol MEA, resulting
in an SRD of 3.7 GJ/tonne CO2 [64,65]. This correlation between the
lean loading and the SRD was also observed in the current work in
Fig. 16.
Two different partial pressures of CO2 (54.6 and 109.4 mbar) in the
inlet flue gas were tested, resulting in rich loadings 0.39 and 0.46 mol
CO2/mol MEA, lean loadings 0.27 and 0.31 mol CO2/mol MEA, and
