SRD 5.01 and 3.98 GJ/tonne CO2 excluding the heat loss [13]. How-
ever, the packing heights of the columns were insufficient to obtain 𝜖
of 90%. Despite the discrepancies in the equipment sizing and process
conditions, the results obtained in the current work agree with previous
such studies.
4.5. Performance of the wash tower
The wash tower plays a crucial role in maintaining the water bal-
ance within the system. The reaction between MEA and CO2 in the flue
gas is exothermic, leading to the release of heat during the absorption
process. This heat causes the water in the solvent to evaporate, which
is carried away with the flue gas. The extent of water evaporation
also depends on the temperature difference between the flue gas at
the absorber inlet and the enriched solvent exiting the absorber [66].
Without an operational wash tower, the evaporated water from the
solvent is lost along with the gas phase. This increases the solvent
concentration and ultimately leads to sub-optimal performance of the
pilot plant. Fig. 34 presents the temperatures of the raw flue gas
entering the absorber and the cleaned gas exiting the absorber.
Fig. 34 shows that the temperature of the cleaned gas exiting the
absorber is consistently higher than that of the raw gas by at least
15 ◦C. The gas entering and leaving the absorber is in a saturated state.
Since the partial pressure of water increases with temperature, it can be
concluded that in the absence of a wash tower, water from the solvent
escapes along with the gas phase. Water loss from the solvent can also
be observed through the sump level in the absorber, as depicted in
Fig. 35(a), and through increasing densities, as shown in Fig. 35(b).
From Fig. 35(a), it is evident that the sump level in the absorber has
decreased from 30% to 0% within 12 hours. Such a drop in sump levels
also indicates foaming in the absorber. To confirm whether foaming is
the cause, the densities of the rich and lean solvents can be examined.
As shown in Fig. 35(b), both the rich and lean densities consistently
increased during the same 12-hour duration mentioned in Fig. 35(a). It
is important to note that no changes were made to any operating pa-
rameters during this period, indicating a steady-state operation. Under
normal conditions, the rich and lean densities would reach a constant
value. Therefore, the continuous increase in densities suggests water
loss from the solvent. A wash tower is crucial to maintain the water
balance in the system. Throughout most of the steady-state operations,
the wash tower was functional. The water balance of the system was
calculated using the following streams:
Fig. 35. Loss of water in the absorber in the absence of the wash tower. (a) Absorber
sump level in the absorber over a period of time. (b) Rich and lean solvent densities
at the same period.
• Water entering the absorber through flue gas: 𝑚H2O,flue gas,in
• Water leaving the wash tower through the lean gas: 𝑚H2O,flue gas,out
• Water leaving the condenser along with the cooled CO2 flow:
𝑚H2O,𝑐
All gas streams are assumed to be saturated with water vapour. The
water balance in the system is determined by calculating the difference
between the inlet and outlet streams. The water balance of the system
for all the steady states is illustrated in Fig. 36.
Fig. 36 shows that water is being added to the system for most
of the steady states. This excess is attributed to the improper control
of the cooling water flow rate in the wash tower. The cooling water
regulates the temperature of the water used in the water wash section.
The control of the cooling water temperature is achieved through the
absorber level. A decrease in the absorber level indicates water loss
from the solvent. As a response, the PI controller increases the cooling
water flow rate, decreasing the water wash temperature and resulting in
condensation of water vapour in the gas stream. This condensed water
is then reintroduced into the absorber. A schematic of the wash tower
operation and control is presented in Fig. 3. The tuning of the cooling

=== PDF p.24 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
24
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. 36. Water balance in the system for all the steady states, calculated as:
𝑚H2O,flue gas,in −𝑚H2O,flue gas,out −𝑚H2O,𝑐.
Fig. 37. Temperature difference between the flue gas entering the absorber and the
clean gas exiting the wash tower for all the steady states.
water PI controller proved challenging due to the time lag between
the reduction in the absorber level and the control of the lean gas
temperature.
Consequently, the exit gas temperature was often cooler than the
inlet flue gas, resulting in the addition of water to the solvent. However,
there are a few cases where the temperature of the lean flue gas is
higher than the inlet gas, leading to water loss. The difference between
the inlet and outlet flue gas temperatures is presented in Fig. 37.
Fig. 37 illustrates the temperature difference between the inlet and
outlet gas temperatures. A positive value indicates the inlet gas was
hotter than the outlet gas, diluting solvent. There are certain steady
states for which solvent dilution was observed as per Fig. 36 despite a
negative 𝛥𝑇value. This is due to the corresponding gas flow rates. The
MEA concentration in the loaded rich and lean solvents is presented in
Fig. 38.
Fig. 38 shows that the MEA concentration in the loaded lean and
rich solvent is consistently below 30 wt%, the initial solvent concentra-
tion. This solvent dilution is a consequence of the wash tower. Dilution
of the solvent is evident from steady states 10 to 40, during which an
excess of water was added from the wash tower. An increase in the
Fig. 38. Concentration of MEA in loaded lean and rich solvents for all the steady
states.
concentration is observed for steady states 40 to 50 due to the loss of
water. High gas flow rates were used at these steady states, and the
spray nozzle at the absorber top was undersized, causing a higher rate
of water loss than water addition. The operation of the wash tower and
the absorber thus influences the solvent concentration.
The control of the wash tower needs further fine-tuning to avoid
dilution or concentration of the solvent. Additionally, the wash tower is
critical to maintaining the water content of the solvent and the absorber
sump levels.
5. Conclusion
This current work analyses pilot-scale tests of CO2 capture con-
ducted at a WtE facility. The steady states obtained will be a baseline
for comparing advanced configurations and new solvent tests. The tests
were performed utilising 30 wt% MEA.
A total of 85 steady-state experiments were conducted by varying
parameters such as reboiler duty, solvent, and gas flow rate. The impact
of the variation of these operating parameters was thoroughly analysed,
leading to recommendations for the optimal operation of such a plant.
CO2 capture flow rates in the range of 5 to 20 kg/h were observed.
A minimum SRD of 3.46 GJ/tonne CO2 was obtained for a gas flow of
113 kg/h, a lean solvent flow of 306 kg/h, reboiler duty of 17.5 kW
excluding the heat loss at the reboiler, and CO2 concentration of
13 vol%. This SRD is lower than the usually observed 3.7 GJ/tonneCO2.
A consequence of changing the reboiler duty is a variation in the
stripping intensity. Changing the stripping intensity was observed to
influence the distribution of energy in the stripper. At low stripping
intensities, more energy is required for sensible heating of the solvent
and at high stripping intensities, steam was lost from the stripper top,
leading to a high SRD value. An optimum stripping intensity in the
range 200 to 220 kJ/kgtotal results in 𝜖of approximately 90% and an
SRD of 3.65 to 3.7 GJ/tonne CO2. At lower stripping intensities (170 to
200 kJ/kgtotal), the SRD increases by 3% due to heat loss in the main
heat exchanger. At higher stripping intensities (220 to 260 kJ/kgtotal),
the SRD is higher than the optimum by 7% due to the steam lost from
the stripper top.
The influence of the L/G ratio on the operation of the absorber
while maintaining a constant 𝜖of 85% was analysed by looking at
the temperature and the CO2 loading profiles along the length of the
column. The packing height in the absorber was effectively utilised
for the cases of L/G = 2.4 and 2.9 kg/kg. For the case of L/G =

=== PDF p.25 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
25
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
3 kg/kg, a significant difference in the loading profile was observed
when compared to 2.9 kg/kg. The L/G term was further reduced to
the ratio of MEA flow in the lean solvent to the CO2 flow in the inlet
flue gas (𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2). An optimal operating point is expected to lie
within 4 to 5 kgMEA∕kgCO2. At low 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 (< 4 kgMEA∕kgCO2), the
cyclic capacity of the solvent needed is high, requiring high stripping
intensities. High stripping intensities lead to energy loss as steam from
the stripper top. At high 𝐿MEA∕𝐺CO2 values (> 5 kgMEA∕kgCO2), the
circulation rate of the solvent is higher, leading to heat loss at the main
heat exchanger.
The influence of the fluid dynamic load in the absorber was analysed
by varying the gas flow rate to the absorber. F-factor was used as the
indicator of fluid dynamic load. An optimum F-factor of 0.79 Pa0.5 was
obtained at which an SRD of 3.4 to 3.5 GJ/tonne CO2 was observed
for 𝜖of 90%. This is lower than the case of 0.72 Pa0.5 by 10%. It
was observed that a lower F-factor requires a higher cyclic capacity.
High cyclic capacity calls for a leaner loading, eventually contributing
to a high SRD value. In contrast, higher F-factors need a lower cyclic
capacity of the solvent due to better wetting of the packing material.
However, higher solvent flow requirements lead to higher sensible
heating necessities. In all the cases of sensitivity analysis, it can be
observed that a stripping intensity of 200–220 kJ/kgtotal gives 𝜖of 90%
irrespective of the solvent or gas flow rate. This parameter could thus
be utilised as an independent factor while operating such a plant.
The performance of the wash tower was also analysed to understand
its role in maintaining the water balance in the system. The wash
tower was cooling the flue gas below its inlet saturation temperature
for most steady states, leading to solvent dilution. On the contrary, in
the absence of the wash tower and without the addition of any make-up
water, the sump level in the absorber was reduced by 30% within 12 h,
highlighting the importance of a functional wash tower for a sustained
and continuous plant operation.
Conducting a parametric analysis of the base case configuration
helps in determining the performance of the pilot and potential oppor-
tunities for optimising 𝜖and energy consumption of the pilot.

Appendix A. Pilot plant description
This section describes the various components of the pilot plant.
A.1. Pilot plant design
The pilot plant was designed considering the capture of CO2 from
flue gas (12 mol%) using 30 wt% MEA as the chemical solvent. The pilot
was designed to capture approximately 1 tonne of CO2 per day. The gas
mole fraction of CO2 is in the order of 12 mol%. This meant that the
absorber had to accommodate approximately 150 Nm3/h gas flow. A
packing was chosen to prevent flooding. The diameter of the column
was fixed at 21.7 cm from a previous biogas project and could not be
changed. The height of the column was decided based on knowledge
from previous lab tests and basic know-how. The residence time was
chosen to be in the order of 10–15 seconds at maximum flow, resulting
in 19.36 m. The reboiler was designed to desorb CO2 at a maximum
SRD of 4 GJ/tonne CO2. Therefore, the total boiler duty was decided to
be 40 kW, accounting for potential heat losses. The pipes were designed
for 1 to 5 m/s for liquid flow and 5 to 15 m/s for gas flow. The pipe
diameters were calculated based on the Reynolds number. The sumps
were designed to accommodate the typical liquid residence time of
4 minu in the columns. This implies that after an initial filling of 60% of
the sump, pumping the liquid for 4 min would result in a new level of
25%, provided the sump volume is in the order of 100 litres. The solvent
flow was determined as approximately 500 kg/h based on mass balance
for a 30 wt% MEA system. The main heat exchanger was designed using
an LMTD of 6.7 K and the maximum liquid flow.
Information on the equipment is summarised in Table A.9.
A.2. Absorber
The absorber is a 19.36 m tall column made of 316 stainless steel
with a diameter of 0.217 m. It has a total effective volume of 778 litres.
The absorber consists of three packing sections, with the bottom two
sections being 5.325 m tall and the third section being 4.35 m. The
flue gas enters the absorber below the bottom packing section, and
feed points are provided above each packing section to introduce lean
solvent. Withdrawal points are located below each packing section
to facilitate solvent extraction. Temperature transmitters are installed
within each internal section, and external temperature sensors are
placed on the surface of the column at various locations. The three
beds used for CO2 capture consist of CMR 0 A Random plastic packing
material. At the top of the column is a smaller packing bed measuring

=== PDF p.26 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
26
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. B.39. Control of various components of the pilot plant.
Fig. C.40. Comparison of expected and measured concentrations of the trace gas with the inlet and outlet probes. (a) Expected vs. measured concentrations. (b) Relative difference
between the expected and measured concentrations.
1.045 m. Above this packing section, a spray nozzle is installed to
introduce water from the wash tower, ensuring water balance in the
solvent. Without a wash tower, this section can add make-up water to
the absorber to compensate for water loss. The topmost bed consists of
Mellapak 250Y structured packing.
Below each packing bed is a liquid collector with a capacity of two
litres. The liquid collector serves multiple purposes, including complete
or partial solvent withdrawals at different packing heights and mixing
the liquid from the overhead packing bed with the feed liquid, which
may have different temperatures and compositions. Above each packing
bed, there is a liquid redistributor. The feed point above each packing
section is connected to the liquid redistributor. Liquid redistributors
ensure uniform liquid distribution of the solvent entering the column,
whether from the feed point or the liquid collector. The liquid redis-
tributor consists of a bucket with holes of a specific size at the base,
allowing for the even distribution of liquid in the subsequent packing
section.
The three packing beds are utilised to capture CO2 from the flue gas.
Liquid samples can be collected at each packing section using sample
lines connected to the liquid collectors, with the samples taken at the
ground level.

=== PDF p.27 ===
Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
27
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. C.41. Comparison of CO2 concentrations between Vaisala probes and ARC data.
The bottom of the column is connected to a sump, which serves as
a storage vessel for the solvent. The sump has a capacity of 120 litres
and is used for collecting the rich solvent, compensating for unsteady
flows through the absorber before entering the stripper. Additionally,
water condensed from the CO2 stream leaving the top of the stripper
is added to the solvent in the absorber sump. The sump levels are also
monitored to detect foaming caused by impurities in the flue gas or loss
of water. The absorber sump has a height of 1.43 m.
By utilising the feed points at each packing section, the effective
height of the absorber can be adjusted from 5.325 m to 15 m.
A.3. Stripper
The stripper column is 17.54 m tall and consists of four packing
sections, with the top three sections measuring 3.5 m in height and the
bottom section measuring 3.4 m. The internals of the stripper column is
similar to those of the absorber column, including feed and withdrawal
points above and below each packing section. Therefore, the effective
height of the stripper can be adjusted from 3.4 m to 13.9 m. The bottom
four beds utilised for stripping consist of Flexiring 5/8 inch random
packing made of 316 stainless steel. The polishing section at the top is
0.5 m high and consists of Mellapak plus 250Y structured packing.
In the stripper, the CO2-rich solvent trickles down the length of
the column and comes in contact with steam rising from the bottom.
The stripper includes a reboiler located at the bottom. Steam required
for stripping CO2 is generated using an electric heating element in the
steam generator. The thermosiphon concept facilitates the steam flow
from the steam generator and condensate flow from the reboiler [67].
The heat duty to the steam generator can be provided as input through
the HMI. The steam generator has a maximum capacity of 40 kW.
The solvent from the stripper enters the reboiler, which is heated
by steam, resulting in a liquid–vapour mixture. The liquid overflows
from the reboiler and enters the flash tank, while the vapour acts as the
stripping agent along the length of the stripper. The flash tank serves
as a storage vessel for the lean solvent and has a capacity of 156 litres.
The level in the flash tank is essential to provide a Net Positive Suction
Head (NPSH) to the lean pump, which circulates the solvent back to
the absorber.
A.4. Heat exchangers
The hot lean solvent from the flash vessel and the cold rich solvent
from the absorber sump is directed through the main HE. This allows

the heat from the lean solvent to be utilised for heating the rich solvent
before it enters the stripper while simultaneously cooling down the lean
solvent before it enters the absorber. A plate heat exchanger with a
maximum heating surface area of 102 m2 is employed for this purpose.
The heat exchanger can handle a maximum temperature of 150 ◦C.
However, a high-pressure drop on the rich side of the solvent in the
heat exchanger can lead to the flashing of CO2 gas, forming gas pockets
within the heat exchanger and potentially reducing its efficiency.
A fusion-bonded plate heat exchanger with a maximum heating
surface area of 1.43 m2 is utilised to cool the lean solvent exiting the
main HE. The temperature of the lean solvent leaving the main HE is
typically in the range of 60–65 ◦C, and it needs to be cooled down to
40 ◦C before entering the absorber. The temperature of the lean solvent

Applied Energy 355 (2024) 122193
28
S.H.B. Vinjarapu et al.
Fig. C.42. Comparison of expected and measured gas flow rates for calibration. (a) Expected vs. measured flow rates. (b) Relative difference between the expected and measured
flow rates.
Fig. D.43. Process flow diagram of the pilot in base case configuration with the major streams indicated.
plays a crucial role in maintaining the reaction kinetics and controlling
the extent of amine emissions in the gas phase.
The hot CO2–H2O vapour mixture that exits the top of the stripper
column passes through a condenser. The condenser is a plate heat
exchanger with a specific maximum heating surface area of 0.234 m2.
Cooling water is circulated through the condenser to lower the tem-
perature of the gas–vapour mixture. The gas–vapour mixture enters
the condenser at a temperature of 90–100 ◦C and is cooled down to
30 ◦C. Cooling the mixture to lower temperatures reduces water loss
from the system. However, achieving higher cooling requires increased
cooling duties. As a result of the cooling process, the water vapour in
the mixture condenses. The condensed mixture then flows into a gas–
liquid separator, where gravity causes the liquid to settle at the bottom
of the separator. This water is added back to the absorber sump, where
it combines with the rich solvent. Finally, the gas exiting the top of the
separator is released from the pilot plant.
A.5. Solvent circulation
Pumps purchased from Grundfos circulate solvent between the ab-
sorber and stripper, water in the wash column, and other advanced
configurations. The pumps circulating the lean and rich solvent can
pump 1000 kg/h. The life of the pumps depends on the NPSH. To
provide the required NPSH, the absorber sump and the flash tank levels
need to be maintained. At low NPSH values, flashing occurs in the
pumps, which results in cavitation [68], and a drop in the discharge
pressure can be observed. Cavitation was prominently observed in the
case of the rich pump, where the solvent is loaded with CO2 and can
thus quickly flash. Therefore, there is a provision on the pumps to vent
out gases that can accumulate due to flashing.
A.6. Solvent filter
An activated carbon filter was installed to continuously clean the
build-up of any heat-stable salts in the solvent. The solvent filter is
positioned after the lean cooler. A 100 kg/h flow rate of lean solvent
through the solvent filter was maintained. The use of lean solvent for
cleaning was preferred over rich solvent to avoid potential flashing
of the rich solvent caused by higher CO2 content and the resulting
pressure drops across the filter.

A.7. Wash tower
The wash tower is a 13.4 m tall column with a total packing height
of 8.69 m. The diameter of the wash tower is 0.215 m. It comprises
six sections, including a demister and five packing beds. The column
internals consists of random packing made of stainless steel, specifically
Koch-Glitsch Flexiring 5/8 inch. The bottom section of the wash tower
features a 1.09 m packed bed, while the demister at the top of the
column is a 0.3 m packing section. Between the demister and the

bottom section, there are four packed beds of 1.9 m packing. The wash
tower is divided into three sections.
A.7.1. Bottom water wash
The two packed sections at the bottom of the wash tower are called
the water wash section. Water is circulated within these two sections
and passed through a heat exchanger for temperature control. The
water wash section serves two purposes: cooling the flue gas exiting
the absorber and cleaning the gas. The chemical absorption of CO2 is
an exothermic process, causing the temperature inside the absorber to
be higher than the temperature of the flue gas entering the absorber.
This temperature difference leads to water loss from the solvent.
The lower part of the wash tower is dedicated to water washing,
where the flue gas is washed with water. The water used for washing
goes through a heat exchanger. A PI controller regulates the cooling
water temperature. The PI controller monitors either the level in the
absorber or the temperature difference between the inlet and outlet
flue gas. A decrease in the absorber level indicates a decrease in water
content. This decrease occurs when the temperature of the flue gas
leaving the absorber is higher than that of the gas entering the absorber.
Monitoring the temperature difference between the inlet and outlet flue
gas is equivalent to monitoring the level in the absorber sump in real-
time. In contrast, the change in the absorber sump level is a delayed
process.
In all base case campaigns, the change in absorber level is used to
control the cooling water flow rate. The PI controller adjusts the cooling
water flow rate to regulate the temperature of the washing water.
Cooling the washing water causes the water carried in the flue gas to
condense. The water wash sump has a level controller that monitors the
water level at the bottom of the wash tower. If the level exceeds the set
point due to condensation, the level controller opens a motorised valve
to return the water to the top of the absorber. A spray nozzle is installed
above the top bed in the absorber to replenish the water in the solvent.
Loss of water from the solvent increases the solvent concentration and
necessitates regular manual addition of makeup water in the absence
of the wash tower.
A.7.2. Acid wash section
As mentioned, the exothermic chemical absorption of CO2 increases
the column’s temperature. Consequently, amine slip along with the
flue gas occurs. There is ongoing discussion regarding the limitation of
amine emissions into the atmosphere. To operate the pilot within these
limits, cleaning the flue gas before it exits the pilot plant and joins the
ARC exhaust line is crucial.
The first step in the cleaning process involves a water wash, which
has been discussed earlier. The bottom two beds of the wash tower were
utilised as the water wash section, and the number of beds used can be
adjusted due to flexible hose connections. After exiting the water wash,
the flue gas enters the acid wash section.
As the name implies, the acid wash section washes the flue gas
with an acid. 0.1 M sulphuric acid was used for this purpose. Since
amines are basic compounds, they can be neutralised by an acid like
sulphuric acid. The pH of the acid is continuously monitored, indicating
the amine accumulation in the acid over time. The acid wash was
primarily used during emission measurements in the gas phase to assess
the impact of the acid and water wash sections.
A.7.3. Polishing section
After the acid wash, the flue gas undergoes one final cleaning step,
which is washed with water in a polishing section. The acid wash and
polishing sections consist of one packing section each to come into
contact with the flue gas. During regular operations without emission
measurements, acid wash is not employed. However, the water wash
and polishing sections are always utilised.

A.8. Cooling water system
The cooling water system comprises a circulation pump and an
air-cooled dry cooler with a cooling duty capacity of 90 kW. The
cooling water is circulated to the lean cooler and the condenser, which
are used to cool the lean solvent and the CO2–H2O vapour streams,
respectively. The lean cooler and the condenser are plate heat ex-
changers, as described in Appendix A.4. The cooling water temperature

Appendix B. Control structure
This section details the control structure of the pilot plant. The
control of various parameters is presented in Fig. B.39.
Fig. B.39 depicts the control structure of the pilot plant. The pilot
plant is capable of running both manually and on automatic control. PI
controllers are installed to manipulate motorised valves and maintain
stable operation and automatic control of the pilot plant. The pilot plant
can also be operated manually by adjusting manual valves to achieve
desired operating conditions.
In Fig. B.39, the red valves depict the motorised valves and the red-
dashed lines indicate the input signal from the PI controller. The PI
controller is given a desired set point and manipulates the correspond-
ing motorised valve based on the actual value read by the transmitter.
Controllers have been installed to adjust several process parameters to
maintain stability. They have been implemented for both the liquid
and gas sides of the system, and they are described in subsequent
sections. There are different ways of controlling the pilot plant. The
depiction provided is the control structure adapted for the base case
campaigns. The control of the components not provided in the structure
was achieved by manual intervention.
B.1. Control of flue gas flow rate
The flue gas flow rate is controlled by a PI controller which ma-
nipulates the impeller speed of the blower. On the other hand, the gas
flow rate can be adjusted manually by providing a specific speed for
the blower.
B.2. Control of rich solvent flow rate
The flow rate of the rich solvent exiting the bottom of the absorber
must be controlled to ensure a consistent flow into the stripper. This is
accomplished through a PI flow controller, which adjusts the motorised
valve position according to the desired set point and the current value.
B.3. Control of absorber sump level
The absorber level must be constant to maintain the required NPSH
for the rich pump. Any deviation of the absorber level from the set point
indicates a loss of solvent caused by foaming or water evaporation.
Conversely, an increased absorber level indicates solvent dilution due
to improper wash tower functioning. The cooling water flow rate in
the water wash section of the wash tower is controlled to maintain the
absorber level, as explained in Appendix A.7. A PI controller regulates
this process.
B.4. Control of flash vessel level
The flash level should be maintained at a sufficient height to ensure
the necessary NPSH for the lean pump while preventing solvent flow
into the Lean vapour compressor. Therefore, the level is controlled by
adjusting the lean solvent flow rate from the vessel into the absorber.

53.2 ± 0.094
B.5. Control of water wash level
A buffer tank is installed at the bottom of the wash column to store
the water used for the water wash. The level of the water wash buffer
tank is monitored using a level indicator. An increased water wash level
indicates water condensation from the lean flue gas. The level in the
water wash tank is regulated by a valve that adjusts the flow of water
from the buffer tank to the top of the absorber. Adding water helps
maintain the absorber’s level and the solvent’s concentration.

If the water used for washing is too cold, a larger amount of water
is condensed and added to the absorber, increasing the absorber level
and dilution of the solvent. Conversely, suppose the water used for
the water wash is too hot. In that case, a smaller amount of water
is condensed and added to the absorber, leading to a decrease in the
absorber level and an increase in the solvent concentration. Therefore,
controlling the cooling water temperature has a cascading effect on
various parameters.

36 ± 0.259
B.6. Control of stripper pressure
The pressure in the stripper is a critical parameter that affects
the reboiler temperature and, consequently, the SRD required for the
process. It is maintained using a PI controller at the condenser’s exit
side. The controller adjusts the flow of the CO2 stream to manipulate
the stripper’s top and bottom pressures. Under steady-state conditions,
the pressure difference along the length of the stripper is less than
0.1 bar. However, when foaming occurs in the stripper, a significant
increase in the bottom pressure is observed, indicating a high-pressure
drop along the stripper.

B.7. Control of reboiler duty
The reboiler duty is controlled by providing a desired load of the
heating element through the HMI.
Appendix C. Calibration
C.1. Calibration of gas flow meters
SITRANS P320 pressure transmitters measured inlet and outlet flue
gas flows. The flowmeters use the pitot measurement principle, where
the flow rate is calculated based on the pressure difference in the pipe.
The inlet and the gas flowmeters were calibrated to ensure the accuracy
of the measurements. The calibration was done by blowing air through
the gas pipes with the inlet and the outlet flowmeters in series. A known
amount of CO2 was injected as a tracer. The concentration of CO2 in
the mixture was measured by CO2 probes placed at the inlet and outlet.
A Coriolis Mass flow controller controlled the mass flow of the tracer.
Therefore, the total mass flow of air could be calculated based on the
flow rate of CO2 and the concentration of CO2. Before calibrating the
flow meters, the CO2 probes were also calibrated.
C.1.1. Calibration of CO2 probes
The CO2 probes utilised for gas flow meters’ calibration were cal-
ibrated. The probes were calibrated by injecting known amounts of
N2 and CO2 using Coriolis mass flow controllers. As the mass flow
rates of the gases are known, the concentration of the gas mixture can
be calculated and compared against the measured concentrations. The
measured concentrations compared to the measured concentrations of
the inlet and outlet probes are presented in Fig. C.40(a).
From Fig. C.40(a), it can be observed that the measured values
are slightly higher than the expected values. However, it can also
be observed that the measured and expected values are in a linear
correlation for both probes. Therefore, the values can be fitted as linear
correlations. The fitted correlations for the inlet and the outlet probes
are as follows:
𝑐𝑖𝑛,𝑒𝑠𝑡[%] = 1.0938 ⋅𝑐𝑖𝑛,𝑚𝑒𝑠
(C.1)
𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑡,𝑒𝑠𝑡[%] = 1.0712 ⋅𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑡,𝑚𝑒𝑠
(C.2)
The relative difference between the fitted concentrations and the mea-
sured values is presented in Fig. C.40(b). From Fig. C.40(b), it can be
observed that the relative difference between the fitted and measured
values is higher than 5% for the inlet probe at low concentrations. For
example, from a concentration of 0.4%, the inlet and outlet probes have
a relative difference in the range ±5%.
The CO2 probes used in the pilot had to be changed several times
during the operational period. The probes are prone to malfunction due
to exposure to condensate in the gas phase. The probes replaced during
the campaign period have not been calibrated. The concentrations
provided by the probe can, be compared to those provided by ARC.
There are two exhaust lines at ARC. The flue gas to the pilot could
be provided through either or both of these exhaust lines. The CO2
concentrations obtained by the Vaisala probes are compared against
the CO2 concentrations of the flue gas exiting both of the exhaust lines.
This comparison is provided in Fig. C.41.
The inlet and outlet gas flow meters were calibrated based on the
CO2 probe calibrations. The calibrated and measured flows and the
relative difference between the two are presented in Figs. C.42(a) and
C.42(b), respectively.
Appendix D. Experimental data
The PFD with tags is presented in Fig. D.43 and the experimental
data in Tables D.10–D.19.
D.1. Guide to the tags
1. TT — Temperature transmitter
2. PT — Pressure transmitter
3. FT — Flow transmitter
4. QT — Concentration transmitter
5. LT — Level transmitter
6. DT — Density measurement
7. TE — Temperature transmitter
8. RD — Reboiler duty
D.2. Guide to the streams
1. G - Gas stream
2. A — Absorber
3. AB — Absorber bed
4. W — Wash column
5. WB — Wash column bed
6. S — Stripper
7. SB — Stripper bed
8. F — Flash vessel
9. C - Condenser
